* What Morocco Was Saying to Spain in Ceuta
  Foreign Policy | 11 Aug 04:01 UTC
  The migrant crisis has underscored Madrid’s increasingly tenuous geopolitical position.
  FULL TEXT: Spanish Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez has built a brand as one of Europe’s most vocal critics of the United States and Israel, recognizing Palestinian statehood over Gaza and denying the U.S. military the use of its Rota and Morón bases during the war against Iran. And while he seems perfectly comfortable playing hardball with governments worlds apart from his own ideology, when it comes to Spain’s fraught relations with Morocco, Sánchez has folded far more than his reputation suggests. Nowhere was that clearer than in his handling of the recent crisis in Ceuta. On July 30, around 70,000 migrants crossed from Morocco into Ceuta, the small Spanish exclave in North Africa, a city of little more than 80,000 inhabitants. The sudden increase in arrivals caught security forces off guard, and local shelters and social services were soon overwhelmed. Lacking food, shelter, or a clear legal path in Spain, most of the migrants headed back to Morocco within a day, their disappointment standing in stark contrast with the joy and chants of “ Viva España ” upon their arrival. At least 88 migrants have died, and more bodies might be recovered from the water.   From refugees to abducted children, Russia has wreaked havoc on young Ukrainians.
  FULL TEXT: Understanding the conflict four years on. As Russian strikes pummel Ukrainian cities, a quieter, systemic crisis also threatens Ukraine’s future. For Russia, targeting Ukrainian youth is not merely collateral damage; it is demographic warfare aimed at erasing Ukrainian identity. For the millions of children forced to grow up in a war zone, the trauma extends far beyond the front lines, stretching from occupied reeducation camps to disrupted homes across the country. Children kidnapped by Russian forces in the occupied territories have received the most global attention. Since Russia’s invasion of Crimea in 2014, and the full-scale attack on Ukraine in 2022, a huge number, possibly into the millions , of Ukrainian children have found themselves living under Russian rule—either in Ukraine or relocated to Russia. There, they have been forced to attend Russian schools and facilities, where they are bombarded with pro-Russian propaganda and drip-fed stories intended to eventually turn them into soldiers for their kidnappers. * Dismantling the ICC Doesn’t Serve U.S. Interests
  Foreign Policy | 10 Aug 10:00 UTC
  The Trump administration’s campaign against the court is transparently cynical.
  FULL TEXT: When we spoke in April with a woman who had survived a Rapid Support Forces (RSF) massacre on the border of Sudan, she did not ask for revenge. For more than two years, the RSF has carried out attacks in the country that amount to crimes against humanity . But instead, the woman asked whether anyone would write down what had happened to her—whether it would be believed, and whether it would matter. That question, repeated to both of us for decades across war zones, is the reason for the International Criminal Court (ICC), established by the Rome Statute in 2002. It is also the reason that our organization, the Reckoning Project , trains citizens to turn what they have survived into evidence that a court can use. And it is precisely the thing that the United States under President Donald Trump has set out to dismantle. That question, repeated to both of us for decades across war zones, is the reason for the International Criminal Court (ICC), established by the Rome Statute in 2002. And it is precisely the thing that the United States under President Donald Trump has set out to dismantle. A few days before International Criminal Justice Day on July 17, U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio announced a campaign to “systematically disable” the ICC. In Rubio’s words, this is the Trump administration’s attempt to demolish the court “ brick by brick ,” supposedly seeking to stop the court from “threatening U.S. sovereignty or targeting Americans.” The Trump administration’s plan promises expanded sanctions against the ICC and any organization or people affiliated with it; visa revocations and travel bans on court staff, which could extend to perceived supporters ; and a diplomatic push to pressure other countries to stop cooperating with the court. * Mister Anthony
  Longreads | 10 Aug 15:30 UTC
  "Mister Anthony could be my grandfather, if my grandfather, a retired Memphis city bus driver, was still alive, still driving strangers from one stop to another."
  FULL TEXT: When Saeed Jones gets a Lyft ride with Mister Anthony in New Orleans, he has no idea he’s in for brief but deep connection that will occupy his mind long after the trip is over. Anthony is a Black elder originally from Mississippi, “old enough to be retired but still taking gigs.” As the man recalls the racism he experienced in the Magnolia State, Jones is reminded of his own experiences, his own trepidation. In all his travels, Jones reveals that Mississippi is the only place he has ever felt scared for his safety as a Black man. Maybe to distract myself from the question of loneliness, I’m working to chart the depths of my ride with Mister Anthony. I’m interested in how people unexpectedly find one another. I refuse to romanticize the labor conditions and racial terror, for example, that played a role in introducing him to me. If America was doing right by someone like Mister Anthony, I would not have met him today. I know that fact like I know the blade of his laughter. But I am grateful I met him. Maybe it’s easier, especially for men of his generation and journey, to set one’s burdens down with a receptive stranger. Or maybe Mister Anthony is just like that with everyone and I happen to be a poet riding co-pilot with deep feelings. I want to save Mister Anthony’s life. I want to make his life better. Writing about him won’t do either, but I bow before him all the same. “A nonfiction account of an American crime.” “In the home of America’s last active tracks, tradition and uncertainty run neck and neck.”
  https://longreads.com/2026/08/10/mississippi-racism-oxford-american/

The post They Swam to Europe but Are Still Stuck on North African Shores ap...
  FULL TEXT: Around Ceuta’s temporary migrant reception center, perched on a hill overlooking the city, hundreds of people lie on the ground waiting for someone else to decide their future. They languish in the summer heat — the combination of the hard North African sun and thick humidity from the Mediterranean is as overwhelming as the cacophony of the cicadas ringing through the eucalyptus forest. Wherever the trees thin looking north, the view of the Spanish peninsula — just 13 miles away — feels like a fever dream: so close, yet hazy and seemingly impossible to reach. But in the immediate moment, there’s something much closer that feels equally impossible: crossing the threshold of the reception center. “My mother has a heart condition and my dad is very old,” says Saad, a 21-year-old Moroccan, his expression clouding with sadness as he speaks about the family he left behind. “I’m here just so I can help my family — to feed them and give them a better life. I want to support them while I still can,” he says. “Everything I earned went straight back to supporting my family, but I struggled just to help them survive. That’s why I decided to come to Ceuta.” Saad is one of the tens of thousands of Moroccans who crossed into Ceuta, the small Spanish enclave bordering Morocco on the shores of North Africa, in a mass movement in late July. Most, like him, hoped that landing in Ceuta would enable them to continue on to Europe in search of better opportunities. Many of them, however, were swiftly returned to Morocco by Spanish authorities. Morocco has made significant economic progress in recent years, but unemployment remains stubbornly high, particularly among young people, and the country struggles to create enough quality jobs. Last year, a wave of protests swept the nation demanding more and better social services and an end to the inequality that was keeping so many Moroccan families struggling to achieve a decent life. Saad said he first learned about the border being open on social media, after posts claiming that Spain would not be able to send back anyone who reached Ceuta by sea spread rapidly across the country. The claims were based on a recent Spanish Supreme Court ruling limiting summary returns of people intercepted at sea. Spanish authorities later stressed that entering Ceuta irregularly does not automatically grant the right to remain in Spain or travel onward to the mainland, and that those who reached the enclave have no automatic pathway to legal status

* Fearing Iran, Saudi Arabia Looks To Draw Turkey and Nuclear-Armed Pakistan Closer

  Last Friday, Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman, Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdoğan and Pakistani Prime Minister Shehbaz Sharif signed a joint defense agreement in Mecca.   FULL TEXT: (Photo by TUR Presidency/Murat Cetinmuhurdar/Anadolu via Getty Images) Last Friday, Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman, Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdoğan and Pakistani Prime Minister Shehbaz Sharif signed a joint defense agreement in Mecca. Commentary has split over whether it represents a serious military commitment or a mere symbolic gesture. The short answer is: It is more serious than many think, for the coming months at least, but unlikely to harden into a durable NATO-like alliance. The context of the signing suggests that Saudi Arabia needs this agreement more than its two partners. Turkey is a NATO member and Pakistan is a nuclear state. Saudi Arabia is neither. More importantly, Riyadh is caught in the unintended consequences of a war on Iran initiated by its traditional security partner, the United States. Since the war began, the kingdom has come under nonstop attack from Iran, pro-Iranian militias in Iraq and, most recently, Yemen’s Houthis. With the Strait of Hormuz closed, Saudi oil exports depend on the Red Sea route through the Bab al-Mandeb; this is the artery the Houthis have declared closed, amounting to a naval blockade of the kingdom. Friday’s agreement in Mecca came just days after Riyadh announced a military alliance to protect navigation in the Red Sea, and one day after a Saudi official publicly warned, citing intelligence, of an imminent coordinated attack by the Houthis and Iraqi militias. Alliance agreements are basically commitments. With no superior authority to enforce them, any party to a treaty can renege. Since states live effectively under political anarchy, then it is reasonable for a country like Iran to be skeptical that such treaties would actually compel a state to come to an ally’s defense. Indeed, Ebrahim Rezaei, a member of the Iranian parliament’s national security committee, has already dismissed the Mecca pact as a “paper agreement” that will bring the Saudis no security. Rezaei’s bravado aside, we know from the academic literature that political leaders normally resort to two main mechanisms to make their commitments credible. The first is raising the cost to themselves of backing down later. When Saudi Arabia, the United Arab Emirates, Egypt and Bahrain announced their boycott of Qatar in 2017, for example, Turkey rushed additional troops to its base in Doha within days. This made any attack on Qatar an attack on Turkish forces as well, and any Turkish retreat from their commitment to defend Qatar fa

* Israel Called Its Beirut Strikes Precise. We Reconstructed Five of Them
  New Lines | 10 Aug 10:29 UTC
  New Lines examined five Israeli airstrikes in and around Beirut, each presented as a targeted operation against a specific individual. The investigation maps a spectrum of destruction — from one man killed while his family survived to two residential buildings reduced to rubble — and asks what accounts for such varied outcomes. The post I...
  FULL TEXT: Israel has repeatedly described its attacks in Lebanon as “precision” or “targeted” strikes against specific Hezbollah officials and military targets. Embedded within those statements is a broader claim: that Israel can identify, locate and distinguish a specific individual from the civilians living around them. A New Lines investigation has tested that claim, by examining in detail five Israeli strikes in and around Beirut in March and April. Together, they reveal a spectrum of destruction — ranging from a single room to two residential buildings — and raise questions about what accounts for those different outcomes. At one end of the spectrum stands a strike in the Mar Takla area of Hazmieh, southeast of Beirut. The attack killed Muhammad Ali Kourani, whom Israel identified as an operative in the Quds Force of Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC), inside a single room, while his wife and children, separated by little more than a wall, survived. In central Beirut’s Zuqaq al-Blat, another strike killed Mohammad Sherri, director of political programming at the Hezbollah-affiliated Al-Manar TV station, along with his wife in their bedroom, while other family members survived. The strike scorched the apartment but left the floors around it intact. In Ain Saadeh, east of Beirut, the intended target of another strike survived, while Pierre and Flavia Moawad, together with their neighbor Roula Mattar, were killed on another floor. Hours earlier, also in Zuquq al-Blat, an overnight strike tore through three floors of a residential building, killing 15-year-old Jad Kobeissi as he slept. At the far end of the spectrum lies an attack in the southern Beirut suburb of Jnah, where a strike reportedly intended to kill a single target largely destroyed two densely populated residential buildings. The question of why the extent of destruction varied so widely carries a particular charge in Lebanon, where neighborhoods are often thought of as belonging to one particular community or another. Mar Takla and Ain Saadeh are predominantly Christian areas; Zuqaq al-Blat and Jnah are not. If the degree of precision were deliberately made higher in some places than in others, the variation would appear less like the contingency of individual operations and more like a judgment made in advance about whose surroundings are more worth sparing. Whether the pattern of these five strikes supports that reading is a question this investigation explores, rather than a premise it

Upholt doesn’t deal in simple explanations; the pleasure, here, is in the complexity. The concept of a “species” is less sturdy than your high school biology teacher might have led you to believe. The most familiar definition is that a species consists of animals that can produce fertile offspring. * The Odyssey Wars
  New Lines | 07 Aug 10:26 UTC
  Nolan's “Odyssey” extracts a universal message from Homer's ancient text and laments a world where rules have collapsed and might makes right. It is a thrilling spectacle and a repudiation of the right’s culture wars. The post The Odyssey Wars appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: … There, in her head of ebony, there was no real need for the historian’s remorse, nor for literature’s. Why not see Helen as the sun saw her, with no Homeric shadow, swinging her plastic sandals on that beach alone, as fresh as the sea-wind? … In the Hindu Kush highlands where I was born, there is no word for beggar. The itinerant mendicants who walk the valley are called “mustafer,” which is Khowar for “traveler.” If one turns up at your door, you offer them food and shelter. The travelers rarely tax your generosity. The same courtesy is extended to any stranger, regardless of standing. The Pashtuns of Pakistan and Afghanistan also have the same tradition. Every Pashtun household with means has a “hujra,” usually a large room where any guest — known or unknown — is offered hospitality. The guests in turn honor the tradition by offering hospitality to others, who may be strangers to them. The tradition is sustained by paying it forward. Ancient Greeks would recognize this tradition. They called it “xenia.” The word is derived from “xenos,” the Greek word for “stranger.” Hospitality to strangers was understood as both a moral obligation and service to the gods. Zeus and Athena were believed to be protectors of strangers. The tradition obliged the host to provide hospitality and the guest to show courtesy and not to abuse the host’s generosity. The tradition was sustained by the same combination of reciprocity and paying forward. Such traditions have all but disappeared from the West. There are individual acts of generosity, but nothing that is sustained by a shared code. Indeed, over the past decade, the stranger has become a subject of hostility and derision — and not just for the far right. Before his defenestration, former U.K. Prime Minister Keir Starmer had mined xenophobia for political gain by lamenting Britain’s decline into an “island of strangers.” The word “xenophobia” is derived from the same root as “xenia.” In adapting Homer’s epic “The Odyssey,” Christopher Nolan has chosen to use xenia as the moral pivot of the story. Except that Nolan broadens it into what he calls “Zeus’ law,” which he equates to the golden rule: Treat others as you’d have them treat you. In doing so, he makes the principle universal, accessible to more than an exclusive club of classicists, while also making the epic’s final irruption of violence less incomprehensible to a modern audience. Ancient Greek traditions of hospitality may not be familiar to the average cinemag
  https://newlinesmag.com/argument/the-odyssey-wars/

Marriage ceremonies are no longer what they once were—nor do they express hope in quite the same way—but they have become powerful testaments to resilience. As I worked on this story, I came to understand that the war has not only transformed weddings in Gaza. It has transformed people’s relationship with time itself. Before the war, marriage followed a familiar sequence. People finished their education, found work, built or rented a home, saved enough money and only then began planning a wedding. Families often postponed marriages until those expectations had been met. But now no one has that luxury — the luxury of time. I spoke with one mother who had lost several of her children during the war, yet hurried to arrange the marriage of her youngest son. She told me she had always dreamed, like any mother, of watching all of her children grow up, get married, and fill her home with grandchildren. On March 26, 2013, a military force from the 61st Brigade of Bashar al-Assad’s army stormed the village of Saida al-Golan (also known as Saida al-Hanout) in Quneitra governorate in southern Syria, striking terror into the residents of the small village and its surroundings. Although local sources disagreed at the time about the exact number of victims, all confirmed that a “massacre” had taken place, witnessed by residents of the village that lies just over a mile east of the buffer zone with the Israeli-occupied Golan Heights. Those sources also confirmed that regime forces had thrown the dead bodies into a nearby valley. From that point on, as the regime’s military campaigns intensified across the country, and as its forces and allies committed dozens of documented massacres, the massacre at Wadi Tuaim was set aside. Neither the victims nor their families were granted any measure of justice against the perpetrators who had killed their sons. But the fall of the Assad regime has finally given them the chance to raise their voices and demand the justice they have sought for 13 years, and to tell a story whose details they whispered among themselves for fear of the deposed regime’s retribution. This investigation by SIRAJ reveals for the first time the names and faces of two officers of the former regime’s army accused of being responsible for the Wadi Tuaim massacre, in which 13 people have been confirmed killed, nine of them from a single family, including a child.   FULL TEXT: Governments that deploy internet shutdowns to thwart protesters have struggled to clamp down on offline messaging apps. Last month, the app had its biggest real-world test after authorities shut down internet access during student protests in New Delhi. As protesters turned to Bitchat, the Indian government tried to block access to the app’s source code on GitHub, a Microsoft-owned platform that allows developers to create, store, manage, and share their code. In the past, the Indian government has blocked apps from official app stores due to various reasons, but digital rights advocates say this appears to be the first known attempt to geoblock an open-source software repository. The episode highlights a growing challenge for governments. Across the globe, state-enforced internet blackouts have become a primary weapon against protests and independent journalism during political crises. Beyond silencing dissent, these measures serve to block real-time documentation and evade international scrutiny of official misconduct. Apps built to function without the internet make those tactics less effective. “The decentralized architecture of the app means that there is no central server or database and therefore there cannot be a central kill switch,” Namrata Maheshwari, senior policy counsel and encryption policy lead at digital civil rights nonprofit Access Now, told Rest of World . “Downloaded apps will continue to work as long as they are within the physical proximity required for the mesh network to function.” Bitchat reached an all-time high of 430,000 daily active users in India on July 26, according to market intelligence firm Sensor Tower. Between July 20 and July 26, India accounted for 74% of the app’s global downloads. During Hong Kong’s 2014 protests, FireChat became a popular way for demonstrators to communicate without mobile networks. More recently, Bitchat downloads spiked during protests in Myanmar, Nepal, Uganda, Madagascar, and Iran. In February, voters elected Laura Fernández, the tough-on-crime heir to outgoing president Rodrigo Chaves, who was constitutionally barred from seeking reelection. Then June arrived with two successive bangs. Keiko Fujimori, daughter of the late president Alberto Fujimori — convicted of crimes against humanity for creating and authorizing death squads that targeted civilians — was elected president of Peru. Then, in Colombia, Abelardo de la Espriella, a flamboyant businessperson, former defense lawyer for paramilitaries and antiestablishment libertarian, defeated Iván Cepeda, the senator who was supposed to carry Gustavo Petro’s progressive agenda forward. It was no longer just the Southern Cone; the Andes now belonged to the right as well. These governments join a growing trend across the region: Daniel Noboa in Ecuador, Nayib Bukele in El Salvador , José Raúl Mulino in Panama, Santiago Peña in Paraguay and, to some extent, Rodrigo Paz in Bolivia. Although these leaders are not united around a distinct ideological core, they all lean to the right to varying degrees. Brazil may follow in October this year, when Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva (“Lula”) , the veteran leader of the Brazilian left, will face Flávio Bolsonaro — son of former president Jair Bolsonaro, who was convicted last year of plotting a coup to remain in power after losing the 2022 election. What explains this shift to the right across the region? Commentators offer two answers, and both, I believe, are incomplete.   FULL TEXT: At the turn of the millennium, India became the global hub for medical transcription. With fiber optic cables laid in the 1990s, American hospitals found it cheap and efficient to beam voice files across the ocean. Indian workers, fluent in English and medical terminology, turned dictations into polished reports overnight. The time-zone gap became an advantage: a surgeon in Boston could dictate before bed, and wake up to flawless notes typed in Bengaluru. By the early 2000s, the industry seemed secure. Companies built training academies, invested in IT systems and promised young recruits that this was a career with a future. But when I met Aakash (name changed) in June 2024, that promise had already unravelled. At 25, he was clinging to a short-term contract in a Bengaluru firm. He had joined a year earlier, after months of training. During recruitment, he asked the CEO whether AI posed a threat. ‘At least five years away,’ came the reassurance. Yet, by March 2024, as the financial year closed, American clients began to vanish. Contracts were cancelled or shifted to automated platforms. Hundreds of jobs disappeared almost overnight. The cafeteria that once buzzed at 3 a.m. emptied out. Hiring froze; the recruitment team itself was laid off. New employees were brought in under “conditional retention training,” only to be dismissed weeks later (an accounting trick to show inflated headcounts to prospective clients) and workers lived in limbo. Economists have a phrase for jobs like Aakash’s: high exposure , low complementarity. Which means that these are roles where machines can easily take over, rather than raising the human’s capacity to work. In Manila, tens of thousands of Filipino transcriptionists have already been displaced . In Nairobi, call center operators now compete with chatbots for contracts that once fed families. In Colombia, customer support roles vanish into generative systems. The pattern is the same: codified knowledge that once built middle-class livelihoods is being swallowed whole by automation. Even the people training the systems feel the blade at their neck. Contractors working on products like Gemini and AI Overviews describe a familiar dread: they are annotating their own obsolescence. Unlike earlier waves of mechanization that chipped away at manual labour, these systems target the cognitive core of professional life. The scale of this change is huge. His departure comes as the ICC continues handling cases involving some of the world’s most powerful leaders, including Russian President Vladimir Putin and Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu. At the same time, the court is facing pressure from governments that question its authority, threaten its operations and argue that it has overstepped its mandate. The ICC has always been controversial. Every prosecutor has left behind a complicated legacy. The court’s first prosecutor, Luis Moreno Ocampo, was accused of focusing too heavily on Africa, leading many to believe that the ICC was only targeting leaders from the continent. His successor, Fatou Bensouda, spent much of her term in office trying to move beyond that criticism while facing resistance over investigations involving Afghanistan and Palestine. Karim Khan expanded the court’s reach with some of its most consequential cases, including the arrest warrant issued for Putin and the case against Netanyahu. But his departure has created another crisis for an institution already struggling with questions about its credibility. The next prosecutor will inherit a court under unprecedented political pressure. The United States and Israel have intensified efforts to undermine the institution, while some member states have questioned whether the court can continue to function without stronger political support. At the same time, millions of victims of atrocities around the world continue to see the ICC as a necessary check on governments that fail to hold their own leaders accountable. The choice of chief prosecutor will be important, but it will not resolve the deeper questions facing the institution, which revolve around whether the ICC can maintain its independence while operating in a world shaped by power politics. For some observers, however, the attacks against the ICC are not evidence of failure.   FULL TEXT: Jan. 13, 2010, is a day that I will never forget. That Wednesday morning, I had several missed calls, text messages and notifications on my phone. I did not know it at the time, but the day before, a massive earthquake had struck near the capital city of Haiti, Port-au-Prince. As the child of Haitian immigrants who came to the United States in 1980, I have always kept up with events in Haiti, and this was, without question, the most devastating natural disaster that had occurred on the island in my lifetime. Hundreds of thousands of Haitians died as a result of the magnitude 7 earthquake, and millions more were severely impacted. At that point, I didn’t know all this — I was concerned for my family. Many of my cousins, uncles and aunts were still in Haiti. Were they okay? What would they do now that the capital had been damaged so severely? Where would they find refuge? On Jan. 15, in the midst of the chaos and confusion, the United States government extended temporary protected status (TPS) to Haitian nationals, giving them the ability to live and work in the United States without fear of deportation. Now, after 15 years of Haitians building their lives, integrating into their local communities and contributing to the economic and social fabric of the United States, the second Trump administration has abruptly ended TPS for Haiti and has plunged communities across the nation into a state of fear, anxiety and despair. Our industries, our hospitals, our nursing homes, our businesses and the entire U.S. economy will be harmed by this decision. TPS was established with the passage of the Immigration Act of 1990. The law significantly reshaped our immigration system and provided additional pathways for immigrants to come to the U.S. It raised the number of immigrants allowed to enter each year from 290,000 to 675,000 and introduced various programs, such as the Diversity Visa lottery and the H-1B Skilled Worker Visa Program, to increase the number of diverse, skilled workers entering the country. Along with these changes to the U.S. immigration system, this law established the TPS program, which, in this instance, provided work permits and protection for Haitians living in the U.S. after the earthquake. TPS designations are applied to countries that are experiencing extremely challenging circumstances, such as “on-going armed conflict, an environmental disaster, or extraordinary and temporary conditions,” as stated by the U.S. Department of Homeland Security.

* Magical Mystery Toads
  Longreads | 04 Aug 10:00 UTC
  The long, tangled, and mostly wrong history of the psychedelic toad.
  FULL TEXT: Kimon de Greef | Longreads | August 4, 2026 | 4,741 words (17 minutes) From The Ego Trip: Psychedelic Toads, a Trail of Deaths, and the Guru Who Peddled Transcendence by Kimon de Greef. Copyright © 2026 by Kimon de Greef. Excerpted by permission of Doubleday, an imprint of The Knopf Doubleday Group, a division of Penguin Random House LLC. All rights reserved. In 1935, two chemists at Tokyo’s Imperial University synthesized a new molecule and unwittingly launched one of the strangest narratives of the psychedelic age. Toshio Hoshino and Kenya Shimodaira were working on a family of compounds known as the tryptamines, which occur widely in nature and have a range of intriguing biological properties. The neurotransmitter serotonin, which among other things regulates mood, cognition, and social behavior, is a tryptamine. So are many psychoactive drugs, although this would only be discovered later on. The Japanese scientists had recently completed the world’s first laboratory synthesis of a molecule called bufotenine, which is produced by toads and certain species of mushrooms. Now, via a sequence of reactions, they engineered a close chemical relative. Diagrams of the two tryptamines looked nearly identical, each represented by two angular rings and a tail, resembling microscopic organisms; the only difference lay in the shape of the head. Such tiny changes in chemical structure “can lead to radically different consequences, like a railroad’s switch points,” as the writer and organic chemist Primo Levi once wrote. The new molecule’s name, following scientific convention, would be 5-MeO-DMT. Hoshino and Shimodaira published their findings in the Bulletin of the Chemical Society of Japan, leaving no explanation as to why they had conducted these experiments. The early 20th century was a time of wide-ranging exploration in organic chemistry, with researchers synthesizing new compounds to assess their pharmacological potential or better understand the reactions by which one substance could be transformed into another. Many of these compounds had no evident utility, such as 5-MeO-DMT, which for decades was thought only to be a product of the laboratory. This changed in 1959, when the molecule was isolated from a shrub native to Brazil. Not long afterward, 5-MeO-DMT was detected in Anadenanthera peregrina, a leguminous tree from the Caribbean and South America. For thousands of years, Indigenous communities had been harvesting its slender bean pods to prepare snuffs
